Question 2(b) (ii

Check whether the following graph is bipartite graph.

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {1, 2,3, 4,5, 6}. A graph is said to be bipartite graph if its vertex
set can be partitioned in two disjoined sets V;, V2 such that every edge has one end point in V; and other
one in V2. Let the partition of the vertex set is given as

Vi = {1,2,6} and V2 = {3,4,5} [2 Marks]

Further, the edges are {1,3}, {1,4}, {1,5}, {2,3}, {2,4}, {2, 5}, {6,3}, {6,4}, {6,5}

It is evident that every edge has one end point in V; and other one in V2, then by definition, G is a
bipartite graph. [1 Mark]

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 4 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove that 𝑅
is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 4. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 4𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 4(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 4𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 4𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 4𝑘1 + 4𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 4(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
1M
Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 𝑛(𝑛+ 1)(𝑛+ 2) is a multiple of 6 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: : Let P(n) denotes the statement that we are supposed to prove. We prove the statement by
induction. We first show that P(1) is true:
Step-1:
P(1): 1(2)(3) = 6, which is divisible by 6 and therefore P(1) is true. (1.5M)
Step-2:
Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k. That is,
k(k+1)(k+2) is divisible by 6.   (1.5M)
Step-3:
To prove: P(k+1) is true.
Note that,
(k+1)( k+2)( k+3) = k(k+1)( k+2) + 3(k+1)( k+2)
Therefore, (k+1)( k+2)( k+3) is divisible by 6 iff k(k+1)( k+2) and 3(k+1)( k+2)
Both are divisible by 6. From inductive hypothesis (our assumption in Step 2) k(k+1)( k+2) is
divisible by 6. Hence it remains to show that 3(k+1)( k+2) is divisible by 6, which is equivalent to
show that (k+1)( k+2) is divisible by 2. But this is always true as one of the two consecutive numbers
will be even.   (3M)
This completes the proof.
Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first vertex and then in
sequential order assign C 1 to each vertex which is not adjacent to a
previous vertex which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2 and the
subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a fourth-colour
C 4 and so an until all the vertices are coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for solution 3
marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
********** 4 **********

Q.3 c) In a survey of 60 people, it was found that; 25 read Business India, 26 reads India Today, 26
reads Times of India, 11 reads both Business India and India Today, 09 reads both Business India
and Times of India, 08 reads both India Today and Times of India, 08 reads none of the three. Then
find (i) How many read all three? (ii) How many read exactly one?
Solution: : Let B, I and T denotes the sets of people reading Business India, India Today, and Times of
India respectively. Then we have,
|B| = 25, |I| = 26, |T| = 26,
|B⋂I| = 11, |B⋂T| = 9, |I⋂T| = 8,
Since 8 people read none of the newspaper, the number of people reading at least one newspaper are
|B ⋃ I ⋃ T| = 60 −8 = 52.
Now using the formula:
|B ⋃ I ⋃ T| = |B| + |I| + |T| −|B⋂I| −|B⋂T| −|I⋂T| + |B⋂I⋂T|
(1M)
We have:
52 = 25 + 26 + 26 −11 −9 −8 + |B⋂I⋂T|
Which implies |B⋂I⋂T| = 52 −49 = 3, that settles the first question.   (1.5M)
For the second question the answer is
|B| + |I| + |T| −2(|B⋂I| + |B⋂T| + |I⋂T|) + 3|B⋂I⋂T|   (𝟏. 𝟓𝑴)
= 25 + 26 + 26 −2(11 + 9 + 8) + 3(3)
= 30.   (𝟐𝑴)
The idea to the last part is: add each set individually and then since (B⋂T), (B⋂I), (I⋂T) got added twice, remove 2 times these
numbers. Now while adding B, I, and T, we added the intersection B⋂I⋂T thrice while removing 2 times the pairwise intersection
we removed total 6 times B⋂I⋂T. Therefore to offset the error, we need to add 3 times B⋂I⋂T and that is the formula provided
above. Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Question 7(c) (i)

Determine whether the given graph has an Eulerian path or an Eulerian circuit, [fan Eulerian path or
circuit exists, write one such path or circuit explicitly.

O-1)—-®)
a

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {a,.y,2,8.u,0,0}. It can be seen that deg(a) = 3degly) =
S,deg(z) = 4, deg(s) = 2,deg(u) = 3,deg(v) = 2,degiw) = 2. We know that A connected graph with at
least two vertices has an Eulerian circuit if and only if each of its vertices has even degree, As G is a
connected graph (every pair of vertices can be reached from the other by traversing a sequence of edges)
and there are two vertices af odd degree (y and uj, the graph G can mot have Eulerian circuit, [2 Marks]

Further, A commected graph has an Eulerian path but not an Eulerian circuit if amd only if it has
exactly two vertices of odd degree. As G has exactly two vertices of odd degree, there exists an Eulerian
path (which is not Eulerian ecireuit), {1 Mark)
If we consider the path P = (u,v,mw,u,2,3,y,2,2,y) then, it covers all of the edges of G exactly once
(Path P has different starting and terminating vertices). Consequently, path P is an Eulerian path which
is not Eulerian circuit. {1 Mark)

Question 2(b) (ii

Check whether the following graph is bipartite graph.

Solution

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 4 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove that 𝑅
is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 4. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 4𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 4(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 4𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 4𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 4𝑘1 + 4𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 4(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
1M
Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 𝑛(𝑛+ 1)(𝑛+ 2) is a multiple of 6 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: : Let P(n) denotes the statement that we are supposed to prove. We prove the statement by
induction. We first show that P(1) is true:
Step-1:
P(1): 1(2)(3) = 6, which is divisible by 6 and therefore P(1) is true. (1.5M)
Step-2:
Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k. That is,
k(k+1)(k+2) is divisible by 6.   (1.5M)
Step-3:
To prove: P(k+1) is true.
Note that,
(k+1)( k+2)( k+3) = k(k+1)( k+2) + 3(k+1)( k+2)
Therefore, (k+1)( k+2)( k+3) is divisible by 6 iff k(k+1)( k+2) and 3(k+1)( k+2)
Both are divisible by 6. From inductive hypothesis (our assumption in Step 2) k(k+1)( k+2) is
divisible by 6. Hence it remains to show that 3(k+1)( k+2) is divisible by 6, which is equivalent to
show that (k+1)( k+2) is divisible by 2. But this is always true as one of the two consecutive numbers
will be even.   (3M)
This completes the proof.
Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first vertex and then in
sequential order assign C 1 to each vertex which is not adjacent to a
previous vertex which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2 and the
subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a fourth-colour
C 4 and so an until all the vertices are coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for solution 3
marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
Q.3 c) In a survey of 60 people, it was found that; 25 read Business India, 26 reads India Today, 26
reads Times of India, 11 reads both Business India and India Today, 09 reads both Business India
and Times of India, 08 reads both India Today and Times of India, 08 reads none of the three. Then
find (i) How many read all three? (ii) How many read exactly one?
Solution: : Let B, I and T denotes the sets of people reading Business India, India Today, and Times of
India respectively. Then we have,
|B| = 25, |I| = 26, |T| = 26,
|B⋂I| = 11, |B⋂T| = 9, |I⋂T| = 8,
Since 8 people read none of the newspaper, the number of people reading at least one newspaper are
|B ⋃ I ⋃ T| = 60 −8 = 52.
Now using the formula:
|B ⋃ I ⋃ T| = |B| + |I| + |T| −|B⋂I| −|B⋂T| −|I⋂T| + |B⋂I⋂T|
(1M)
We have:
52 = 25 + 26 + 26 −11 −9 −8 + |B⋂I⋂T|
Which implies |B⋂I⋂T| = 52 −49 = 3, that settles the first question.   (1.5M)
For the second question the answer is
|B| + |I| + |T| −2(|B⋂I| + |B⋂T| + |I⋂T|) + 3|B⋂I⋂T|   (𝟏. 𝟓𝑴)
= 25 + 26 + 26 −2(11 + 9 + 8) + 3(3)
= 30.   (𝟐𝑴)
The idea to the last part is: add each set individually and then since (B⋂T), (B⋂I), (I⋂T) got added twice, remove 2 times these
numbers. Now while adding B, I, and T, we added the intersection B⋂I⋂T thrice while removing 2 times the pairwise intersection
we removed total 6 times B⋂I⋂T. Therefore to offset the error, we need to add 3 times B⋂I⋂T and that is the formula provided
above. Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

circuit exists, write one such path or circuit explicitly.

O-1)—-®)
a
(w)

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {a,.y,2,8.u,0,0}. It can be seen that deg(a) = 3degly) =

least two vertices has an Eulerian circuit if and only if each of its vertices has even degree, As G is a
connected graph (every pair of vertices can be reached from the other by traversing a sequence of edges)
and there are two vertices af odd degree (y and uj, the graph G can mot have Eulerian circuit, [2 Marks]

Further, A commected graph has an Eulerian path but not an Eulerian circuit if amd only if it has
exactly two vertices of odd degree. As G has exactly two vertices of odd degree, there exists an Eulerian
path (which is not Eulerian ecireuit), {1 Mark)
If we consider the path P = (u,v,mw,u,2,3,y,2,2,y) then, it covers all of the edges of G exactly once
(Path P has different starting and terminating vertices). Consequently, path P is an Eulerian path which
is not Eulerian circuit. {1 Mark)

Question 2(b) (ii

Check whether the following graph is bipartite graph.

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {1, 2,3, 4,5, 6}. A graph is said to be bipartite graph if its vertex
set can be partitioned in two disjoined sets V;, V2 such that every edge has one end point in V; and other
one in V2. Let the partition of the vertex set is given as

Vi = {1,2,6} and V2 = {3,4,5} [2 Marks]

It is evident that every edge has one end point in V; and other one in V2, then by definition, G is a
bipartite graph. [1 Mark]

Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 𝑛(𝑛+ 1)(𝑛+ 2) is a multiple of 6 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: : Let P(n) denotes the statement that we are supposed to prove. We prove the statement by
induction. We first show that P(1) is true:
Step-1:
P(1): 1(2)(3) = 6, which is divisible by 6 and therefore P(1) is true. (1.5M)
Step-2:
Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k. That is,
k(k+1)(k+2) is divisible by 6.   (1.5M)
Step-3:
To prove: P(k+1) is true.
Note that,
(k+1)( k+2)( k+3) = k(k+1)( k+2) + 3(k+1)( k+2)
Therefore, (k+1)( k+2)( k+3) is divisible by 6 iff k(k+1)( k+2) and 3(k+1)( k+2)
Both are divisible by 6. From inductive hypothesis (our assumption in Step 2) k(k+1)( k+2) is
divisible by 6. Hence it remains to show that 3(k+1)( k+2) is divisible by 6, which is equivalent to
show that (k+1)( k+2) is divisible by 2. But this is always true as one of the two consecutive numbers
will be even.   (3M)
This completes the proof.

sequential order assign C 1 to each vertex which is not adjacent to a
previous vertex which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2 and the
subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a fourth-colour
C 4 and so an until all the vertices are coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for solution 3
marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).

Determine whether the given graph has an Eulerian path or an Eulerian circuit, [fan Eulerian path or
circuit exists, write one such path or circuit explicitly.

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {a,.y,2,8.u,0,0}. It can be seen that deg(a) = 3degly) =
S,deg(z) = 4, deg(s) = 2,deg(u) = 3,deg(v) = 2,degiw) = 2. We know that A connected graph with at
least two vertices has an Eulerian circuit if and only if each of its vertices has even degree, As G is a
connected graph (every pair of vertices can be reached from the other by traversing a sequence of edges)
and there are two vertices af odd degree (y and uj, the graph G can mot have Eulerian circuit, [2 Marks]

Further, A commected graph has an Eulerian path but not an Eulerian circuit if amd only if it has
exactly two vertices of odd degree. As G has exactly two vertices of odd degree, there exists an Eulerian
path (which is not Eulerian ecireuit), {1 Mark)
If we consider the path P = (u,v,mw,u,2,3,y,2,2,y) then, it covers all of the edges of G exactly once
(Path P has different starting and terminating vertices). Consequently, path P is an Eulerian path which
is not Eulerian circuit. {1 Mark)

Question 2(b) (ii

Check whether the following graph is bipartite graph.

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {1, 2,3, 4,5, 6}. A graph is said to be bipartite graph if its vertex
set can be partitioned in two disjoined sets V;, V2 such that every edge has one end point in V; and other
one in V2. Let the partition of the vertex set is given as

Vi = {1,2,6} and V2 = {3,4,5} [2 Marks]

Further, the edges are {1,3}, {1,4}, {1,5}, {2,3}, {2,4}, {2, 5}, {6,3}, {6,4}, {6,5}

It is evident that every edge has one end point in V; and other one in V2, then by definition, G is a
bipartite graph. [1 Mark]

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 4 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove that 𝑅
is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 4. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 4𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 4(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 4𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 4𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 4𝑘1 + 4𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 4(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
1M
Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 𝑛(𝑛+ 1)(𝑛+ 2) is a multiple of 6 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: : Let P(n) denotes the statement that we are supposed to prove. We prove the statement by
induction. We first show that P(1) is true:
Step-1:
P(1): 1(2)(3) = 6, which is divisible by 6 and therefore P(1) is true. (1.5M)
Step-2:
Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k. That is,
k(k+1)(k+2) is divisible by 6.   (1.5M)
Step-3:
To prove: P(k+1) is true.
Note that,
(k+1)( k+2)( k+3) = k(k+1)( k+2) + 3(k+1)( k+2)
Therefore, (k+1)( k+2)( k+3) is divisible by 6 iff k(k+1)( k+2) and 3(k+1)( k+2)
Both are divisible by 6. From inductive hypothesis (our assumption in Step 2) k(k+1)( k+2) is
divisible by 6. Hence it remains to show that 3(k+1)( k+2) is divisible by 6, which is equivalent to
show that (k+1)( k+2) is divisible by 2. But this is always true as one of the two consecutive numbers
will be even.   (3M)
This completes the proof.
Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first vertex and then in
sequential order assign C 1 to each vertex which is not adjacent to a
previous vertex which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2 and the
subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a fourth-colour
C 4 and so an until all the vertices are coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for solution 3
marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
Q.3 c) In a survey of 60 people, it was found that; 25 read Business India, 26 reads India Today, 26
reads Times of India, 11 reads both Business India and India Today, 09 reads both Business India
and Times of India, 08 reads both India Today and Times of India, 08 reads none of the three. Then
find (i) How many read all three? (ii) How many read exactly one?
Solution: : Let B, I and T denotes the sets of people reading Business India, India Today, and Times of
India respectively. Then we have,
|B| = 25, |I| = 26, |T| = 26,
|B⋂I| = 11, |B⋂T| = 9, |I⋂T| = 8,
Since 8 people read none of the newspaper, the number of people reading at least one newspaper are
|B ⋃ I ⋃ T| = 60 −8 = 52.
Now using the formula:
|B ⋃ I ⋃ T| = |B| + |I| + |T| −|B⋂I| −|B⋂T| −|I⋂T| + |B⋂I⋂T|
(1M)
We have:
52 = 25 + 26 + 26 −11 −9 −8 + |B⋂I⋂T|
Which implies |B⋂I⋂T| = 52 −49 = 3, that settles the first question.   (1.5M)
For the second question the answer is
|B| + |I| + |T| −2(|B⋂I| + |B⋂T| + |I⋂T|) + 3|B⋂I⋂T|   (𝟏. 𝟓𝑴)
= 25 + 26 + 26 −2(11 + 9 + 8) + 3(3)
= 30.   (𝟐𝑴)
The idea to the last part is: add each set individually and then since (B⋂T), (B⋂I), (I⋂T) got added twice, remove 2 times these
numbers. Now while adding B, I, and T, we added the intersection B⋂I⋂T thrice while removing 2 times the pairwise intersection
we removed total 6 times B⋂I⋂T. Therefore to offset the error, we need to add 3 times B⋂I⋂T and that is the formula provided
above. Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Question 2(b) (ii

Check whether the following graph is bipartite graph.

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {1, 2,3, 4,5, 6}. A graph is said to be bipartite graph if its vertex
set can be partitioned in two disjoined sets V;, V2 such that every edge has one end point in V; and other
one in V2. Let the partition of the vertex set is given as

Vi = {1,2,6} and V2 = {3,4,5} [2 Marks]

Further, the edges are {1,3}, {1,4}, {1,5}, {2,3}, {2,4}, {2, 5}, {6,3}, {6,4}, {6,5}

It is evident that every edge has one end point in V; and other one in V2, then by definition, G is a
bipartite graph. [1 Mark]

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 4 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove that 𝑅
is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 4. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 4𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 4(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 4𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 4𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 4𝑘1 + 4𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 4(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
1M
Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 𝑛(𝑛+ 1)(𝑛+ 2) is a multiple of 6 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: : Let P(n) denotes the statement that we are supposed to prove. We prove the statement by
induction. We first show that P(1) is true:
Step-1:
P(1): 1(2)(3) = 6, which is divisible by 6 and therefore P(1) is true. (1.5M)
Step-2:
Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k. That is,
k(k+1)(k+2) is divisible by 6.   (1.5M)
Step-3:
To prove: P(k+1) is true.
Note that,
(k+1)( k+2)( k+3) = k(k+1)( k+2) + 3(k+1)( k+2)
Therefore, (k+1)( k+2)( k+3) is divisible by 6 iff k(k+1)( k+2) and 3(k+1)( k+2)
Both are divisible by 6. From inductive hypothesis (our assumption in Step 2) k(k+1)( k+2) is
divisible by 6. Hence it remains to show that 3(k+1)( k+2) is divisible by 6, which is equivalent to
show that (k+1)( k+2) is divisible by 2. But this is always true as one of the two consecutive numbers
will be even.   (3M)
This completes the proof.
Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first vertex and then in
sequential order assign C 1 to each vertex which is not adjacent to a
previous vertex which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2 and the
subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a fourth-colour
C 4 and so an until all the vertices are coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for solution 3
marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
Q.3 c) In a survey of 60 people, it was found that; 25 read Business India, 26 reads India Today, 26
reads Times of India, 11 reads both Business India and India Today, 09 reads both Business India
and Times of India, 08 reads both India Today and Times of India, 08 reads none of the three. Then
find (i) How many read all three? (ii) How many read exactly one?
Solution: : Let B, I and T denotes the sets of people reading Business India, India Today, and Times of
India respectively. Then we have,
|B| = 25, |I| = 26, |T| = 26,
|B⋂I| = 11, |B⋂T| = 9, |I⋂T| = 8,
Since 8 people read none of the newspaper, the number of people reading at least one newspaper are
|B ⋃ I ⋃ T| = 60 −8 = 52.
Now using the formula:
|B ⋃ I ⋃ T| = |B| + |I| + |T| −|B⋂I| −|B⋂T| −|I⋂T| + |B⋂I⋂T|
(1M)
We have:
52 = 25 + 26 + 26 −11 −9 −8 + |B⋂I⋂T|
Which implies |B⋂I⋂T| = 52 −49 = 3, that settles the first question.   (1.5M)
For the second question the answer is
|B| + |I| + |T| −2(|B⋂I| + |B⋂T| + |I⋂T|) + 3|B⋂I⋂T|   (𝟏. 𝟓𝑴)
= 25 + 26 + 26 −2(11 + 9 + 8) + 3(3)
= 30.   (𝟐𝑴)
The idea to the last part is: add each set individually and then since (B⋂T), (B⋂I), (I⋂T) got added twice, remove 2 times these
numbers. Now while adding B, I, and T, we added the intersection B⋂I⋂T thrice while removing 2 times the pairwise intersection
we removed total 6 times B⋂I⋂T. Therefore to offset the error, we need to add 3 times B⋂I⋂T and that is the formula provided
above. Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Q.1 a). Check, whiter the following relation on the set of all people is reflective and transitive
relation. Give the argument to support your answer. 𝑅= {(𝑎, 𝑏)| 𝑎 and 𝑏 have met}
Solution: Reflexivity: (𝑎, 𝑎) , a has definitely met himself/herself , thus reflexivity holds [1.5m]
Transitivity: (𝑎, 𝑏), (𝑏, 𝑐), (𝑎, 𝑐). 𝑎 has met 𝑏, 𝑏 has met 𝑐, but it is not necessary that 𝑎
may have met 𝑐. Thus transitivity does not hold and the relation is not an equivalence relation.
[2.5m]

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 7 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove
that 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 7. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 7(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘1 + 7𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 7(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
1M
Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 8𝑛−3𝑛 is multiple of 5 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: Step-1: To show P(1) is true.
We know that 8-3 = 5, divisible by 5. (1.5M)
Step-2: Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k > 0. (1M)
Step-3: To prove that P(k+1) is true.
Note that
8𝑘+1 − 3𝑘+1 = 8. 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= (5 + 3). 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= 5. 8𝑘+ 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘). (2M)
Using Step-2, we get that 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘) is divisible by 5 and 5. 8𝑘 is anyway divisible by 5 and therefore
their sum is divisible by 5. (1.5M)
This completes the proof.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm
and write steps of the algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to
decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first
vertex and then in sequential order assign C 1 to each
vertex which is not adjacent to a previous vertex
which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2
and the subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a
fourth-colour C 4 and so an until all the vertices are
coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for
solution 3 marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
Q.3 c) Among the integers 1 and 300,
1. How many of them are divisible by 3 or 5 or 7?
2. How many are not divisible by 3 nor by 5 and nor by 7?
Solution: Let A, B and C denote the sets of numbers between 1 and 300 that are divisible by 3, 5
and 7 respectively. Therefore, we have
|A| = ⌊300/3⌋= 100
|B| = ⌊300/5⌋= 60
|C| = ⌊300/7⌋= 42
|A⋂B| = ⌊300/15⌋= 20
|A⋂C| = ⌊300/21⌋= 14
|B⋂C| = ⌊300/35⌋= 8
|A⋂B⋂C| = ⌊300/105⌋= 2
(3 Marks)
Then answer to the first question is
|A⋃B⋃C| = |A| + |B| + |C| −|B⋂A| −|A⋂C| −|B⋂C| + |B⋂A⋂C|
|A⋃B⋃C| = 100 + 60 + 42 −20 −14 −8 + 2
|A⋃B⋃C| = 162
(3 Marks)
The answer to the second question is 300 – 162 = 138.
(2 Marks)
Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Q.5 c) Construct minimum spanning tree using Prim’s algorithm. Also write the steps of the
algorithm and find minimum total weight.
Solution: Here is the algorithm:
1) Set S = .
1) Pick any vertex in the graph. Update S
2) Add the minimum edge incident to that vertex to S.
3) Continue to add edges into S (n-2 more times) using the
following rule:
Add the minimum edge weight to S that is incident to S
but that doesn't form a cycle when added to S.
Total weight = 17

least two vertices has an Eulerian circuit if and only if each of its vertices has even degree. As Gisa Q mor fey |

Q.1 a). Check, whiter the following relation on the set of all people is reflective and transitive
relation. Give the argument to support your answer. 𝑅= {(𝑎, 𝑏)| 𝑎 and 𝑏 have met}
Solution: Reflexivity: (𝑎, 𝑎) , a has definitely met himself/herself , thus reflexivity holds [1.5m]
Transitivity: (𝑎, 𝑏), (𝑏, 𝑐), (𝑎, 𝑐). 𝑎 has met 𝑏, 𝑏 has met 𝑐, but it is not necessary that 𝑎
may have met 𝑐. Thus transitivity does not hold and the relation is not an equivalence relation.
[2.5m]

degree in the graph must be (3 – 1) = 2, but the maximum degree in the sequence is 4.

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 7 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove
that 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 7. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 7(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘1 + 7𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 7(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
1M
Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 8𝑛−3𝑛 is multiple of 5 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: Step-1: To show P(1) is true.
We know that 8-3 = 5, divisible by 5. (1.5M)
Step-2: Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k > 0. (1M)
Step-3: To prove that P(k+1) is true.
Note that
8𝑘+1 − 3𝑘+1 = 8. 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= (5 + 3). 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= 5. 8𝑘+ 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘). (2M)
Using Step-2, we get that 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘) is divisible by 5 and 5. 8𝑘 is anyway divisible by 5 and therefore
their sum is divisible by 5. (1.5M)
This completes the proof.

Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm
and write steps of the algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to
decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first
vertex and then in sequential order assign C 1 to each
vertex which is not adjacent to a previous vertex
which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2
and the subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a
fourth-colour C 4 and so an until all the vertices are
coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for
solution 3 marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
Q.3 c) Among the integers 1 and 300,
1. How many of them are divisible by 3 or 5 or 7?
2. How many are not divisible by 3 nor by 5 and nor by 7?
Solution: Let A, B and C denote the sets of numbers between 1 and 300 that are divisible by 3, 5
and 7 respectively. Therefore, we have
|A| = ⌊300/3⌋= 100
|B| = ⌊300/5⌋= 60
|C| = ⌊300/7⌋= 42
|A⋂B| = ⌊300/15⌋= 20
|A⋂C| = ⌊300/21⌋= 14
|B⋂C| = ⌊300/35⌋= 8
|A⋂B⋂C| = ⌊300/105⌋= 2
(3 Marks)
Then answer to the first question is
|A⋃B⋃C| = |A| + |B| + |C| −|B⋂A| −|A⋂C| −|B⋂C| + |B⋂A⋂C|
|A⋃B⋃C| = 100 + 60 + 42 −20 −14 −8 + 2
|A⋃B⋃C| = 162
(3 Marks)
The answer to the second question is 300 – 162 = 138.
(2 Marks)
Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Q.5 c) Construct minimum spanning tree using Prim’s algorithm. Also write the steps of the
algorithm and find minimum total weight.
Solution: Here is the algorithm:
1) Set S = .
1) Pick any vertex in the graph. Update S
2) Add the minimum edge incident to that vertex to S.
3) Continue to add edges into S (n-2 more times) using the
following rule:
Add the minimum edge weight to S that is incident to S
but that doesn't form a cycle when added to S.
Total weight = 17
1) Algorithm has (4 marks)
2) 4 marks for graph and answer.

Question 7(c) (i)

Determine whether the given graph has an Eulerian path or an Eulerian circuit. If an Eulerian path or
circuit exists, write one such path or circuit explicitly. ‘

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {z,y, 2,8,u,v,w}. It can be seen that deg(x) = 2,deg(y) =
3, deg(z) = 4, deg(s) = 3, deg(u) = 4, deg(v) = 2,deg(w) = 2. We know that A connected graph with at
least two vertices has an Eulerian circuit if and only if each of its vertices has even degree. As Gisa Q mor fey |
connected graph (every pair of vertices can be reached from the other by traversing a sequence of edges) !
and there are two vertices of odd degree (y and s), the graph G can not have Eulerian circuit. J

Further, A connected graph has an Eulerian path but not an Eulerian circuit if and only if it has] j

exactly two vertices of odd degree. As G has exactly two vertices of odd degree, there exists an Eulerian !
path (which is not Eulerian circuit). am aly

If we consider the path P = (y,2,2,y, 8,2, u,v, w, 8) then, it covers all of the edges of G exactly

once (Path P has different starting and terminating vertices). Consequently, path P is an Eulerian path
which is not Eulerian circuit. 4

Question 7(c) (ii)

Provide an example of a graph that possesses both an Eulerian circuit and a Hamiltonian circuit, and
explain why the graph satisfies the conditions for both types of circuits. Additionally, give a non-trivial
example of a graph that has neither an Eulerian circuit nor a Hamiltonian circuit, and explain-clearly
why it fails to meet the requirements for both circuits. -

Solution

A graph G said to have an Eulerian circuit if there exists a simple circuit which covers all the edges (i.e.
a closed path which covers all the edges of graph G exactly once) and a Hamiltonian circuit in a: graph
G is a simple circuit that passes through every vertex of G exactly once.

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 7 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove
that 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 7. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.

(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘1 + 7𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 7(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M

Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 8𝑛−3𝑛 is multiple of 5 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: Step-1: To show P(1) is true.
We know that 8-3 = 5, divisible by 5. (1.5M)
Step-2: Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k > 0. (1M)
Step-3: To prove that P(k+1) is true.
Note that
8𝑘+1 − 3𝑘+1 = 8. 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= (5 + 3). 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= 5. 8𝑘+ 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘). (2M)
Using Step-2, we get that 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘) is divisible by 5 and 5. 8𝑘 is anyway divisible by 5 and therefore
their sum is divisible by 5. (1.5M)
This completes the proof.

Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm
and write steps of the algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to
decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first
vertex and then in sequential order assign C 1 to each
vertex which is not adjacent to a previous vertex
which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2
and the subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a
fourth-colour C 4 and so an until all the vertices are
coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for
solution 3 marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
Q.3 c) Among the integers 1 and 300,
1. How many of them are divisible by 3 or 5 or 7?
2. How many are not divisible by 3 nor by 5 and nor by 7?
Solution: Let A, B and C denote the sets of numbers between 1 and 300 that are divisible by 3, 5
and 7 respectively. Therefore, we have
|A| = ⌊300/3⌋= 100
|B| = ⌊300/5⌋= 60
|C| = ⌊300/7⌋= 42
|A⋂B| = ⌊300/15⌋= 20
|A⋂C| = ⌊300/21⌋= 14
|B⋂C| = ⌊300/35⌋= 8
|A⋂B⋂C| = ⌊300/105⌋= 2
(3 Marks)
Then answer to the first question is
|A⋃B⋃C| = |A| + |B| + |C| −|B⋂A| −|A⋂C| −|B⋂C| + |B⋂A⋂C|
|A⋃B⋃C| = 100 + 60 + 42 −20 −14 −8 + 2
|A⋃B⋃C| = 162
(3 Marks)
The answer to the second question is 300 – 162 = 138.
(2 Marks)
Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Q.5 c) Construct minimum spanning tree using Prim’s algorithm. Also write the steps of the
algorithm and find minimum total weight.
Solution: Here is the algorithm:
1) Set S = .
1) Pick any vertex in the graph. Update S
2) Add the minimum edge incident to that vertex to S.
3) Continue to add edges into S (n-2 more times) using the
following rule:
Add the minimum edge weight to S that is incident to S
but that doesn't form a cycle when added to S.
Total weight = 17

Question 7(c) (i)

Determine whether the given graph has an Eulerian path or an Eulerian circuit. If an Eulerian path or
circuit exists, write one such path or circuit explicitly. ‘

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {z,y, 2,8,u,v,w}. It can be seen that deg(x) = 2,deg(y) =
3, deg(z) = 4, deg(s) = 3, deg(u) = 4, deg(v) = 2,deg(w) = 2. We know that A connected graph with at
least two vertices has an Eulerian circuit if and only if each of its vertices has even degree. As Gisa Q mor fey |
connected graph (every pair of vertices can be reached from the other by traversing a sequence of edges) !
and there are two vertices of odd degree (y and s), the graph G can not have Eulerian circuit. J

Further, A connected graph has an Eulerian path but not an Eulerian circuit if and only if it has] j

exactly two vertices of odd degree. As G has exactly two vertices of odd degree, there exists an Eulerian !
path (which is not Eulerian circuit). am aly

If we consider the path P = (y,2,2,y, 8,2, u,v, w, 8) then, it covers all of the edges of G exactly

once (Path P has different starting and terminating vertices). Consequently, path P is an Eulerian path
which is not Eulerian circuit. 4

Question 7(c) (ii)

explain why the graph satisfies the conditions for both types of circuits. Additionally, give a non-trivial
example of a graph that has neither an Eulerian circuit nor a Hamiltonian circuit, and explain-clearly
why it fails to meet the requirements for both circuits. -

A graph G said to have an Eulerian circuit if there exists a simple circuit which covers all the edges (i.e.
a closed path which covers all the edges of graph G exactly once) and a Hamiltonian circuit in a: graph
G is a simple circuit that passes through every vertex of G exactly once.

Q.1 a). Check, whiter the following relation on the set of all people is reflective and transitive
relation. Give the argument to support your answer. 𝑅= {(𝑎, 𝑏)| 𝑎 and 𝑏 have met}
Solution: Reflexivity: (𝑎, 𝑎) , a has definitely met himself/herself , thus reflexivity holds [1.5m]
Transitivity: (𝑎, 𝑏), (𝑏, 𝑐), (𝑎, 𝑐). 𝑎 has met 𝑏, 𝑏 has met 𝑐, but it is not necessary that 𝑎
may have met 𝑐. Thus transitivity does not hold and the relation is not an equivalence relation.
[2.5m]

2 M

vertices in G = 6. Maximum degree of any vertex in a simple graph is = n – 1 = 6 – 1 = 5.
But the maximum degree in the given degree sequence is 7, therefore the given degree
sequence is not graphic. Second degree sequence contains 3 vertices. The maximum
degree in the graph must be (3 – 1) = 2, but the maximum degree in the sequence is 4.
Hence, the given degree sequences are not graphic. 2 M

Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 8𝑛−3𝑛 is multiple of 5 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: Step-1: To show P(1) is true.
We know that 8-3 = 5, divisible by 5. (1.5M)
Step-2: Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k > 0. (1M)
Step-3: To prove that P(k+1) is true.
Note that
8𝑘+1 − 3𝑘+1 = 8. 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= (5 + 3). 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= 5. 8𝑘+ 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘). (2M)
Using Step-2, we get that 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘) is divisible by 5 and 5. 8𝑘 is anyway divisible by 5 and therefore
their sum is divisible by 5. (1.5M)
This completes the proof.

Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to

vertex and then in sequential order assign C 1 to each
vertex which is not adjacent to a previous vertex
which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2
and the subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a
fourth-colour C 4 and so an until all the vertices are
coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for
solution 3 marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).

and 7 respectively. Therefore, we have
|A| = ⌊300/3⌋= 100
|B| = ⌊300/5⌋= 60
|C| = ⌊300/7⌋= 42
|A⋂B| = ⌊300/15⌋= 20
|A⋂C| = ⌊300/21⌋= 14
|B⋂C| = ⌊300/35⌋= 8
|A⋂B⋂C| = ⌊300/105⌋= 2
(3 Marks)
Then answer to the first question is
|A⋃B⋃C| = |A| + |B| + |C| −|B⋂A| −|A⋂C| −|B⋂C| + |B⋂A⋂C|
|A⋃B⋃C| = 100 + 60 + 42 −20 −14 −8 + 2
|A⋃B⋃C| = 162
(3 Marks)
The answer to the second question is 300 – 162 = 138.
(2 Marks)
Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Q.5 c) Construct minimum spanning tree using Prim’s algorithm. Also write the steps of the
algorithm and find minimum total weight.
Solution: Here is the algorithm:
1) Set S = .
1) Pick any vertex in the graph. Update S
2) Add the minimum edge incident to that vertex to S.
3) Continue to add edges into S (n-2 more times) using the
following rule:
Add the minimum edge weight to S that is incident to S
but that doesn't form a cycle when added to S.
Total weight = 17
1) Algorithm has (4 marks)
2) 4 marks for graph and answer.

Question 7(c) (i)

Determine whether the given graph has an Eulerian path or an Eulerian circuit. If an Eulerian path or
circuit exists, write one such path or circuit explicitly. ‘

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {z,y, 2,8,u,v,w}. It can be seen that deg(x) = 2,deg(y) =
3, deg(z) = 4, deg(s) = 3, deg(u) = 4, deg(v) = 2,deg(w) = 2. We know that A connected graph with at
least two vertices has an Eulerian circuit if and only if each of its vertices has even degree. As Gisa Q mor fey |
connected graph (every pair of vertices can be reached from the other by traversing a sequence of edges) !
and there are two vertices of odd degree (y and s), the graph G can not have Eulerian circuit. J

Further, A connected graph has an Eulerian path but not an Eulerian circuit if and only if it has] j

exactly two vertices of odd degree. As G has exactly two vertices of odd degree, there exists an Eulerian !
path (which is not Eulerian circuit). am aly

If we consider the path P = (y,2,2,y, 8,2, u,v, w, 8) then, it covers all of the edges of G exactly

once (Path P has different starting and terminating vertices). Consequently, path P is an Eulerian path
which is not Eulerian circuit. 4

Question 7(c) (ii)

Provide an example of a graph that possesses both an Eulerian circuit and a Hamiltonian circuit, and
explain why the graph satisfies the conditions for both types of circuits. Additionally, give a non-trivial
example of a graph that has neither an Eulerian circuit nor a Hamiltonian circuit, and explain-clearly
why it fails to meet the requirements for both circuits. -

Solution

A graph G said to have an Eulerian circuit if there exists a simple circuit which covers all the edges (i.e.
a closed path which covers all the edges of graph G exactly once) and a Hamiltonian circuit in a: graph
G is a simple circuit that passes through every vertex of G exactly once.

Q.1 a). Check, whiter the following relation on the set of all people is reflective and transitive
relation. Give the argument to support your answer. 𝑅= {(𝑎, 𝑏)| 𝑎 and 𝑏 have met}
Solution: Reflexivity: (𝑎, 𝑎) , a has definitely met himself/herself , thus reflexivity holds [1.5m]
Transitivity: (𝑎, 𝑏), (𝑏, 𝑐), (𝑎, 𝑐). 𝑎 has met 𝑏, 𝑏 has met 𝑐, but it is not necessary that 𝑎
may have met 𝑐. Thus transitivity does not hold and the relation is not an equivalence relation.
[2.5m]

Q.1 d) (i) Give an example of a graph that is regular, but not complete, with each vertex having
degree three.
(ii) Show if the degree sequences (2,3,4,5,6,7) and (2,2,4) are graphic.
Solution: (i)
2 M
(ii) In the first sequence, the number of odd vertices is 3 i.e., odd and the number of
vertices in G = 6. Maximum degree of any vertex in a simple graph is = n – 1 = 6 – 1 = 5.
But the maximum degree in the given degree sequence is 7, therefore the given degree
sequence is not graphic. Second degree sequence contains 3 vertices. The maximum
degree in the graph must be (3 – 1) = 2, but the maximum degree in the sequence is 4.
Hence, the given degree sequences are not graphic. 2 M

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 7 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove
that 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 7. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 7(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘1 + 7𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 7(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
1M
Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 8𝑛−3𝑛 is multiple of 5 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: Step-1: To show P(1) is true.
We know that 8-3 = 5, divisible by 5. (1.5M)
Step-2: Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k > 0. (1M)
Step-3: To prove that P(k+1) is true.
Note that
8𝑘+1 − 3𝑘+1 = 8. 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= (5 + 3). 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= 5. 8𝑘+ 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘). (2M)
Using Step-2, we get that 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘) is divisible by 5 and 5. 8𝑘 is anyway divisible by 5 and therefore
their sum is divisible by 5. (1.5M)
This completes the proof.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm
and write steps of the algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to
decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first
vertex and then in sequential order assign C 1 to each
vertex which is not adjacent to a previous vertex
which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2
and the subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a
fourth-colour C 4 and so an until all the vertices are
coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for
solution 3 marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
Q.3 c) Among the integers 1 and 300,
1. How many of them are divisible by 3 or 5 or 7?
2. How many are not divisible by 3 nor by 5 and nor by 7?
Solution: Let A, B and C denote the sets of numbers between 1 and 300 that are divisible by 3, 5
and 7 respectively. Therefore, we have
|A| = ⌊300/3⌋= 100
|B| = ⌊300/5⌋= 60
|C| = ⌊300/7⌋= 42
|A⋂B| = ⌊300/15⌋= 20
|A⋂C| = ⌊300/21⌋= 14
|B⋂C| = ⌊300/35⌋= 8
|A⋂B⋂C| = ⌊300/105⌋= 2
(3 Marks)
Then answer to the first question is
|A⋃B⋃C| = |A| + |B| + |C| −|B⋂A| −|A⋂C| −|B⋂C| + |B⋂A⋂C|
|A⋃B⋃C| = 100 + 60 + 42 −20 −14 −8 + 2
|A⋃B⋃C| = 162
(3 Marks)
The answer to the second question is 300 – 162 = 138.
(2 Marks)
Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

1) Set S = .
1) Pick any vertex in the graph. Update S
2) Add the minimum edge incident to that vertex to S.
3) Continue to add edges into S (n-2 more times) using the
following rule:
Add the minimum edge weight to S that is incident to S
but that doesn't form a cycle when added to S.
Total weight = 17

Question 7(c) (i)

Determine whether the given graph has an Eulerian path or an Eulerian circuit. If an Eulerian path or
circuit exists, write one such path or circuit explicitly. ‘

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {z,y, 2,8,u,v,w}. It can be seen that deg(x) = 2,deg(y) =
3, deg(z) = 4, deg(s) = 3, deg(u) = 4, deg(v) = 2,deg(w) = 2. We know that A connected graph with at
least two vertices has an Eulerian circuit if and only if each of its vertices has even degree. As Gisa Q mor fey |
connected graph (every pair of vertices can be reached from the other by traversing a sequence of edges) !
and there are two vertices of odd degree (y and s), the graph G can not have Eulerian circuit. J

Further, A connected graph has an Eulerian path but not an Eulerian circuit if and only if it has] j

exactly two vertices of odd degree. As G has exactly two vertices of odd degree, there exists an Eulerian !
path (which is not Eulerian circuit). am aly

If we consider the path P = (y,2,2,y, 8,2, u,v, w, 8) then, it covers all of the edges of G exactly

once (Path P has different starting and terminating vertices). Consequently, path P is an Eulerian path
which is not Eulerian circuit. 4

Question 7(c) (ii)

Provide an example of a graph that possesses both an Eulerian circuit and a Hamiltonian circuit, and
explain why the graph satisfies the conditions for both types of circuits. Additionally, give a non-trivial
example of a graph that has neither an Eulerian circuit nor a Hamiltonian circuit, and explain-clearly
why it fails to meet the requirements for both circuits. -

Solution

A graph G said to have an Eulerian circuit if there exists a simple circuit which covers all the edges (i.e.
a closed path which covers all the edges of graph G exactly once) and a Hamiltonian circuit in a: graph
G is a simple circuit that passes through every vertex of G exactly once.

Q.1 a). Check, whiter the following relation on the set of all people is reflective and transitive
relation. Give the argument to support your answer. 𝑅= {(𝑎, 𝑏)| 𝑎 and 𝑏 have met}
Solution: Reflexivity: (𝑎, 𝑎) , a has definitely met himself/herself , thus reflexivity holds [1.5m]
Transitivity: (𝑎, 𝑏), (𝑏, 𝑐), (𝑎, 𝑐). 𝑎 has met 𝑏, 𝑏 has met 𝑐, but it is not necessary that 𝑎
may have met 𝑐. Thus transitivity does not hold and the relation is not an equivalence relation.
[2.5m]

Solution: (i)

vertices in G = 6. Maximum degree of any vertex in a simple graph is = n – 1 = 6 – 1 = 5.
But the maximum degree in the given degree sequence is 7, therefore the given degree
sequence is not graphic. Second degree sequence contains 3 vertices. The maximum
degree in the graph must be (3 – 1) = 2, but the maximum degree in the sequence is 4.
Hence, the given degree sequences are not graphic. 2 M

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 7 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove
that 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 7. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 7(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘1 + 7𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 7(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
1M
Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 8𝑛−3𝑛 is multiple of 5 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: Step-1: To show P(1) is true.
We know that 8-3 = 5, divisible by 5. (1.5M)
Step-2: Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k > 0. (1M)
Step-3: To prove that P(k+1) is true.
Note that
8𝑘+1 − 3𝑘+1 = 8. 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= (5 + 3). 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= 5. 8𝑘+ 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘). (2M)
Using Step-2, we get that 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘) is divisible by 5 and 5. 8𝑘 is anyway divisible by 5 and therefore
their sum is divisible by 5. (1.5M)
This completes the proof.

Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm
and write steps of the algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to
decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first
vertex and then in sequential order assign C 1 to each
vertex which is not adjacent to a previous vertex
which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2
and the subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a
fourth-colour C 4 and so an until all the vertices are
coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for
solution 3 marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
Q.3 c) Among the integers 1 and 300,
1. How many of them are divisible by 3 or 5 or 7?
2. How many are not divisible by 3 nor by 5 and nor by 7?
Solution: Let A, B and C denote the sets of numbers between 1 and 300 that are divisible by 3, 5
and 7 respectively. Therefore, we have
|A| = ⌊300/3⌋= 100
|B| = ⌊300/5⌋= 60
|C| = ⌊300/7⌋= 42
|A⋂B| = ⌊300/15⌋= 20
|A⋂C| = ⌊300/21⌋= 14
|B⋂C| = ⌊300/35⌋= 8
|A⋂B⋂C| = ⌊300/105⌋= 2
(3 Marks)
Then answer to the first question is
|A⋃B⋃C| = |A| + |B| + |C| −|B⋂A| −|A⋂C| −|B⋂C| + |B⋂A⋂C|
|A⋃B⋃C| = 100 + 60 + 42 −20 −14 −8 + 2
|A⋃B⋃C| = 162
(3 Marks)
The answer to the second question is 300 – 162 = 138.
(2 Marks)
Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 7 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove
that 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 7. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 7(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘1 + 7𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 7(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.

Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 8𝑛−3𝑛 is multiple of 5 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: Step-1: To show P(1) is true.
We know that 8-3 = 5, divisible by 5. (1.5M)
Step-2: Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k > 0. (1M)
Step-3: To prove that P(k+1) is true.
Note that
8𝑘+1 − 3𝑘+1 = 8. 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= (5 + 3). 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= 5. 8𝑘+ 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘). (2M)
Using Step-2, we get that 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘) is divisible by 5 and 5. 8𝑘 is anyway divisible by 5 and therefore
their sum is divisible by 5. (1.5M)
This completes the proof.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm
and write steps of the algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to
decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first
vertex and then in sequential order assign C 1 to each
vertex which is not adjacent to a previous vertex
which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2
and the subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a
fourth-colour C 4 and so an until all the vertices are
coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for
solution 3 marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
Q.3 c) Among the integers 1 and 300,
1. How many of them are divisible by 3 or 5 or 7?
2. How many are not divisible by 3 nor by 5 and nor by 7?
Solution: Let A, B and C denote the sets of numbers between 1 and 300 that are divisible by 3, 5
and 7 respectively. Therefore, we have
|A| = ⌊300/3⌋= 100
|B| = ⌊300/5⌋= 60
|C| = ⌊300/7⌋= 42
|A⋂B| = ⌊300/15⌋= 20
|A⋂C| = ⌊300/21⌋= 14
|B⋂C| = ⌊300/35⌋= 8
|A⋂B⋂C| = ⌊300/105⌋= 2
(3 Marks)
Then answer to the first question is
|A⋃B⋃C| = |A| + |B| + |C| −|B⋂A| −|A⋂C| −|B⋂C| + |B⋂A⋂C|
|A⋃B⋃C| = 100 + 60 + 42 −20 −14 −8 + 2
|A⋃B⋃C| = 162
(3 Marks)
The answer to the second question is 300 – 162 = 138.
(2 Marks)
Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Q.5 c) Construct minimum spanning tree using Prim’s algorithm. Also write the steps of the

Solution: Here is the algorithm:
1) Set S = .

3) Continue to add edges into S (n-2 more times) using the
following rule:
Add the minimum edge weight to S that is incident to S
but that doesn't form a cycle when added to S.
Total weight = 17

Q.1 d) (i) Give an example of a graph that is regular, but not complete, with each vertex having
degree three.
(ii) Show if the degree sequences (2,3,4,5,6,7) and (2,2,4) are graphic.
Solution: (i)
2 M
(ii) In the first sequence, the number of odd vertices is 3 i.e., odd and the number of
vertices in G = 6. Maximum degree of any vertex in a simple graph is = n – 1 = 6 – 1 = 5.
But the maximum degree in the given degree sequence is 7, therefore the given degree
sequence is not graphic. Second degree sequence contains 3 vertices. The maximum
degree in the graph must be (3 – 1) = 2, but the maximum degree in the sequence is 4.
Hence, the given degree sequences are not graphic. 2 M

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 7 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove
that 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 7. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 7(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘1 + 7𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 7(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
1M
Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 8𝑛−3𝑛 is multiple of 5 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: Step-1: To show P(1) is true.
We know that 8-3 = 5, divisible by 5. (1.5M)
Step-2: Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k > 0. (1M)
Step-3: To prove that P(k+1) is true.
Note that
8𝑘+1 − 3𝑘+1 = 8. 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= (5 + 3). 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= 5. 8𝑘+ 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘). (2M)
Using Step-2, we get that 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘) is divisible by 5 and 5. 8𝑘 is anyway divisible by 5 and therefore
their sum is divisible by 5. (1.5M)
This completes the proof.

--- page 6 ---
Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm
and write steps of the algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to
decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first
vertex and then in sequential order assign C 1 to each
vertex which is not adjacent to a previous vertex
which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2
and the subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a
fourth-colour C 4 and so an until all the vertices are
coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for
solution 3 marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
Q.3 c) Among the integers 1 and 300,
1. How many of them are divisible by 3 or 5 or 7?
2. How many are not divisible by 3 nor by 5 and nor by 7?
Solution: Let A, B and C denote the sets of numbers between 1 and 300 that are divisible by 3, 5
and 7 respectively. Therefore, we have
|A| = ⌊300/3⌋= 100
|B| = ⌊300/5⌋= 60
|C| = ⌊300/7⌋= 42
|A⋂B| = ⌊300/15⌋= 20
|A⋂C| = ⌊300/21⌋= 14
|B⋂C| = ⌊300/35⌋= 8
|A⋂B⋂C| = ⌊300/105⌋= 2
(3 Marks)
Then answer to the first question is
|A⋃B⋃C| = |A| + |B| + |C| −|B⋂A| −|A⋂C| −|B⋂C| + |B⋂A⋂C|
|A⋃B⋃C| = 100 + 60 + 42 −20 −14 −8 + 2
|A⋃B⋃C| = 162
(3 Marks)
The answer to the second question is 300 – 162 = 138.
(2 Marks)
Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Q.5 c) Construct minimum spanning tree using Prim’s algorithm. Also write the steps of the
algorithm and find minimum total weight.
Solution: Here is the algorithm:
1) Set S = .
1) Pick any vertex in the graph. Update S
2) Add the minimum edge incident to that vertex to S.
3) Continue to add edges into S (n-2 more times) using the
following rule:
Add the minimum edge weight to S that is incident to S
but that doesn't form a cycle when added to S.
Total weight = 17
1) Algorithm has (4 marks)
2) 4 marks for graph and answer.

Question 7(c) (i)

Determine whether the given graph has an Eulerian path or an Eulerian circuit. If an Eulerian path or
circuit exists, write one such path or circuit explicitly. ‘

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {z,y, 2,8,u,v,w}. It can be seen that deg(x) = 2,deg(y) =
3, deg(z) = 4, deg(s) = 3, deg(u) = 4, deg(v) = 2,deg(w) = 2. We know that A connected graph with at
least two vertices has an Eulerian circuit if and only if each of its vertices has even degree. As Gisa Q mor fey |
connected graph (every pair of vertices can be reached from the other by traversing a sequence of edges) !
and there are two vertices of odd degree (y and s), the graph G can not have Eulerian circuit. J

Further, A connected graph has an Eulerian path but not an Eulerian circuit if and only if it has] j

exactly two vertices of odd degree. As G has exactly two vertices of odd degree, there exists an Eulerian !
path (which is not Eulerian circuit). am aly

If we consider the path P = (y,2,2,y, 8,2, u,v, w, 8) then, it covers all of the edges of G exactly

once (Path P has different starting and terminating vertices). Consequently, path P is an Eulerian path
which is not Eulerian circuit. 4

Question 7(c) (ii)

Provide an example of a graph that possesses both an Eulerian circuit and a Hamiltonian circuit, and
explain why the graph satisfies the conditions for both types of circuits. Additionally, give a non-trivial
example of a graph that has neither an Eulerian circuit nor a Hamiltonian circuit, and explain-clearly
why it fails to meet the requirements for both circuits. -

Solution

A graph G said to have an Eulerian circuit if there exists a simple circuit which covers all the edges (i.e.
a closed path which covers all the edges of graph G exactly once) and a Hamiltonian circuit in a: graph
G is a simple circuit that passes through every vertex of G exactly once.

Q.1 a). Check, whiter the following relation on the set of all people is reflective and transitive
relation. Give the argument to support your answer. 𝑅= {(𝑎, 𝑏)| 𝑎 and 𝑏 have met}
Solution: Reflexivity: (𝑎, 𝑎) , a has definitely met himself/herself , thus reflexivity holds [1.5m]
Transitivity: (𝑎, 𝑏), (𝑏, 𝑐), (𝑎, 𝑐). 𝑎 has met 𝑏, 𝑏 has met 𝑐, but it is not necessary that 𝑎
may have met 𝑐. Thus transitivity does not hold and the relation is not an equivalence relation.
[2.5m]

Q.1 d) (i) Give an example of a graph that is regular, but not complete, with each vertex having
degree three.
(ii) Show if the degree sequences (2,3,4,5,6,7) and (2,2,4) are graphic.
Solution: (i)
2 M
(ii) In the first sequence, the number of odd vertices is 3 i.e., odd and the number of
vertices in G = 6. Maximum degree of any vertex in a simple graph is = n – 1 = 6 – 1 = 5.
But the maximum degree in the given degree sequence is 7, therefore the given degree
sequence is not graphic. Second degree sequence contains 3 vertices. The maximum
degree in the graph must be (3 – 1) = 2, but the maximum degree in the sequence is 4.
Hence, the given degree sequences are not graphic. 2 M

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 7 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove
that 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 7. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 7(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘1 + 7𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 7(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
1M
Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 8𝑛−3𝑛 is multiple of 5 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: Step-1: To show P(1) is true.
We know that 8-3 = 5, divisible by 5. (1.5M)
Step-2: Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k > 0. (1M)
Step-3: To prove that P(k+1) is true.
Note that
8𝑘+1 − 3𝑘+1 = 8. 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= (5 + 3). 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= 5. 8𝑘+ 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘). (2M)
Using Step-2, we get that 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘) is divisible by 5 and 5. 8𝑘 is anyway divisible by 5 and therefore
their sum is divisible by 5. (1.5M)
This completes the proof.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm
and write steps of the algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to
decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first
vertex and then in sequential order assign C 1 to each
vertex which is not adjacent to a previous vertex
which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2
and the subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a
fourth-colour C 4 and so an until all the vertices are
coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for
solution 3 marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
Q.3 c) Among the integers 1 and 300,
1. How many of them are divisible by 3 or 5 or 7?
2. How many are not divisible by 3 nor by 5 and nor by 7?
Solution: Let A, B and C denote the sets of numbers between 1 and 300 that are divisible by 3, 5
and 7 respectively. Therefore, we have
|A| = ⌊300/3⌋= 100
|B| = ⌊300/5⌋= 60
|C| = ⌊300/7⌋= 42
|A⋂B| = ⌊300/15⌋= 20
|A⋂C| = ⌊300/21⌋= 14
|B⋂C| = ⌊300/35⌋= 8
|A⋂B⋂C| = ⌊300/105⌋= 2
(3 Marks)
Then answer to the first question is
|A⋃B⋃C| = |A| + |B| + |C| −|B⋂A| −|A⋂C| −|B⋂C| + |B⋂A⋂C|
|A⋃B⋃C| = 100 + 60 + 42 −20 −14 −8 + 2
|A⋃B⋃C| = 162
(3 Marks)
The answer to the second question is 300 – 162 = 138.
(2 Marks)
Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Q.5 c) Construct minimum spanning tree using Prim’s algorithm. Also write the steps of the
algorithm and find minimum total weight.
Solution: Here is the algorithm:
1) Set S = .
1) Pick any vertex in the graph. Update S
2) Add the minimum edge incident to that vertex to S.
3) Continue to add edges into S (n-2 more times) using the
following rule:
Add the minimum edge weight to S that is incident to S
but that doesn't form a cycle when added to S.
Total weight = 17

Question 7(c) (i)

Determine whether the given graph has an Eulerian path or an Eulerian circuit. If an Eulerian path or
circuit exists, write one such path or circuit explicitly. ‘

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {z,y, 2,8,u,v,w}. It can be seen that deg(x) = 2,deg(y) =
3, deg(z) = 4, deg(s) = 3, deg(u) = 4, deg(v) = 2,deg(w) = 2. We know that A connected graph with at
least two vertices has an Eulerian circuit if and only if each of its vertices has even degree. As Gisa Q mor fey |
connected graph (every pair of vertices can be reached from the other by traversing a sequence of edges) !
and there are two vertices of odd degree (y and s), the graph G can not have Eulerian circuit. J

Further, A connected graph has an Eulerian path but not an Eulerian circuit if and only if it has] j

exactly two vertices of odd degree. As G has exactly two vertices of odd degree, there exists an Eulerian !
path (which is not Eulerian circuit). am aly

If we consider the path P = (y,2,2,y, 8,2, u,v, w, 8) then, it covers all of the edges of G exactly

once (Path P has different starting and terminating vertices). Consequently, path P is an Eulerian path
which is not Eulerian circuit. 4

Question 7(c) (ii)

Provide an example of a graph that possesses both an Eulerian circuit and a Hamiltonian circuit, and
explain why the graph satisfies the conditions for both types of circuits. Additionally, give a non-trivial
example of a graph that has neither an Eulerian circuit nor a Hamiltonian circuit, and explain-clearly
why it fails to meet the requirements for both circuits. -

Solution

A graph G said to have an Eulerian circuit if there exists a simple circuit which covers all the edges (i.e.
a closed path which covers all the edges of graph G exactly once) and a Hamiltonian circuit in a: graph
G is a simple circuit that passes through every vertex of G exactly once.

Q.1 a). Check, whiter the following relation on the set of all people is reflective and transitive
relation. Give the argument to support your answer. 𝑅= {(𝑎, 𝑏)| 𝑎 and 𝑏 have met}
Solution: Reflexivity: (𝑎, 𝑎) , a has definitely met himself/herself , thus reflexivity holds [1.5m]
Transitivity: (𝑎, 𝑏), (𝑏, 𝑐), (𝑎, 𝑐). 𝑎 has met 𝑏, 𝑏 has met 𝑐, but it is not necessary that 𝑎
may have met 𝑐. Thus transitivity does not hold and the relation is not an equivalence relation.
[2.5m]

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 7 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove
that 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 7. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 7(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘1 + 7𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 7(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
1M
Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 8𝑛−3𝑛 is multiple of 5 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: Step-1: To show P(1) is true.
We know that 8-3 = 5, divisible by 5. (1.5M)
Step-2: Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k > 0. (1M)
Step-3: To prove that P(k+1) is true.
Note that
8𝑘+1 − 3𝑘+1 = 8. 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= (5 + 3). 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= 5. 8𝑘+ 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘). (2M)
Using Step-2, we get that 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘) is divisible by 5 and 5. 8𝑘 is anyway divisible by 5 and therefore
their sum is divisible by 5. (1.5M)
This completes the proof.

--- page 6 ---
Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm
and write steps of the algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to
decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first
vertex and then in sequential order assign C 1 to each
vertex which is not adjacent to a previous vertex
which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2
and the subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a
fourth-colour C 4 and so an until all the vertices are
coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for
solution 3 marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
Q.3 c) Among the integers 1 and 300,
1. How many of them are divisible by 3 or 5 or 7?
2. How many are not divisible by 3 nor by 5 and nor by 7?
Solution: Let A, B and C denote the sets of numbers between 1 and 300 that are divisible by 3, 5
and 7 respectively. Therefore, we have
|A| = ⌊300/3⌋= 100
|B| = ⌊300/5⌋= 60
|C| = ⌊300/7⌋= 42
|A⋂B| = ⌊300/15⌋= 20
|A⋂C| = ⌊300/21⌋= 14
|B⋂C| = ⌊300/35⌋= 8
|A⋂B⋂C| = ⌊300/105⌋= 2
(3 Marks)
Then answer to the first question is
|A⋃B⋃C| = |A| + |B| + |C| −|B⋂A| −|A⋂C| −|B⋂C| + |B⋂A⋂C|
|A⋃B⋃C| = 100 + 60 + 42 −20 −14 −8 + 2
|A⋃B⋃C| = 162
(3 Marks)
The answer to the second question is 300 – 162 = 138.
(2 Marks)
Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Q.5 c) Construct minimum spanning tree using Prim’s algorithm. Also write the steps of the
algorithm and find minimum total weight.
Solution: Here is the algorithm:
1) Set S = .
1) Pick any vertex in the graph. Update S
2) Add the minimum edge incident to that vertex to S.
3) Continue to add edges into S (n-2 more times) using the
following rule:
Add the minimum edge weight to S that is incident to S
but that doesn't form a cycle when added to S.
Total weight = 17
1) Algorithm has (4 marks)
2) 4 marks for graph and answer.

Question 7(c) (i)

Determine whether the given graph has an Eulerian path or an Eulerian circuit. If an Eulerian path or
circuit exists, write one such path or circuit explicitly. ‘

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {z,y, 2,8,u,v,w}. It can be seen that deg(x) = 2,deg(y) =
3, deg(z) = 4, deg(s) = 3, deg(u) = 4, deg(v) = 2,deg(w) = 2. We know that A connected graph with at
least two vertices has an Eulerian circuit if and only if each of its vertices has even degree. As Gisa Q mor fey |
connected graph (every pair of vertices can be reached from the other by traversing a sequence of edges) !
and there are two vertices of odd degree (y and s), the graph G can not have Eulerian circuit. J

Further, A connected graph has an Eulerian path but not an Eulerian circuit if and only if it has] j

exactly two vertices of odd degree. As G has exactly two vertices of odd degree, there exists an Eulerian !
path (which is not Eulerian circuit). am aly

If we consider the path P = (y,2,2,y, 8,2, u,v, w, 8) then, it covers all of the edges of G exactly

once (Path P has different starting and terminating vertices). Consequently, path P is an Eulerian path
which is not Eulerian circuit. 4

Question 7(c) (ii)

Provide an example of a graph that possesses both an Eulerian circuit and a Hamiltonian circuit, and
explain why the graph satisfies the conditions for both types of circuits. Additionally, give a non-trivial
example of a graph that has neither an Eulerian circuit nor a Hamiltonian circuit, and explain-clearly
why it fails to meet the requirements for both circuits. -

Solution

A graph G said to have an Eulerian circuit if there exists a simple circuit which covers all the edges (i.e.
a closed path which covers all the edges of graph G exactly once) and a Hamiltonian circuit in a: graph
G is a simple circuit that passes through every vertex of G exactly once.

Q.2 c) If 𝑅 is the relation defined on ℤ (set of integer), such that, 𝑚𝑅𝑛 ⇔ 7 divides (𝑚−𝑛). Prove
that 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
Solution:
(1) 𝑚−𝑚= 0 and 0 is divided by 7. Hence 𝑚𝑅𝑚 ∀ 𝑚∈ℤ, i.e. 𝑅 is reflesxive.
1.5 M
(2) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 i.e. 𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘 for some 𝑘∈ℤ⇒𝑛−𝑚= 7(−𝑘) ⇒𝑛𝑅𝑚. Hence 𝑅 is symmetric. 1.5M
(3) If 𝑚𝑅𝑛 (𝑚−𝑛= 7𝑘1 ⋯(𝑖)) and 𝑛𝑅𝑡 (𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘2 ⋯(𝑖𝑖)) for some 𝑘1 & 𝑘2 ∈ℤ by (𝑖) + (𝑖𝑖), we have
𝑚−𝑛+ 𝑛−𝑡= 7𝑘1 + 7𝑘2 ⇒𝑚−𝑡= 7(𝑘1 + 𝑘2) ⇒𝑚𝑅𝑡. Hence 𝑅 is transitive.   2 M
By (1) , (2) and (3) 𝑅 is an equivalence relation.
1M
Q.3 a) Prove by mathematical induction that 8𝑛−3𝑛 is multiple of 5 for 𝑛≥1.
Solution: Step-1: To show P(1) is true.
We know that 8-3 = 5, divisible by 5. (1.5M)
Step-2: Assume that P(k) is true for some natural number k > 0. (1M)
Step-3: To prove that P(k+1) is true.
Note that
8𝑘+1 − 3𝑘+1 = 8. 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= (5 + 3). 8𝑘−3. 3𝑘= 5. 8𝑘+ 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘). (2M)
Using Step-2, we get that 3. (8𝑘− 3𝑘) is divisible by 5 and 5. 8𝑘 is anyway divisible by 5 and therefore
their sum is divisible by 5. (1.5M)
This completes the proof.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
Q.3 b) Determine the chromatic number of the graph given below using Welch Powell’s algorithm
and write steps of the algorithm.
Solution: Welch-Powell Algorithm:
Step 1: Order the vertices of G according to
decreasing degrees.
Step 2: Assign the first colour to C 1 , to the first
vertex and then in sequential order assign C 1 to each
vertex which is not adjacent to a previous vertex
which was assigned the colour C 1 .
Step 3: Repeat the Step 2, with a second-colour C 2
and the subsequence of non-coloured vertices.
Step 4: Repeat Step 3, with a third-colour C 3 , then a
fourth-colour C 4 and so an until all the vertices are
coloured.
Step 5: Exit.   (For algorithm 3 marks and for
solution 3 marks)
We can see that using Welsh Powell’s algorithm we can color the vertices with only 3 types of colors
(chromatic number of this graph is 3).
Q.3 c) Among the integers 1 and 300,
1. How many of them are divisible by 3 or 5 or 7?
2. How many are not divisible by 3 nor by 5 and nor by 7?
Solution: Let A, B and C denote the sets of numbers between 1 and 300 that are divisible by 3, 5
and 7 respectively. Therefore, we have
|A| = ⌊300/3⌋= 100
|B| = ⌊300/5⌋= 60
|C| = ⌊300/7⌋= 42
|A⋂B| = ⌊300/15⌋= 20
|A⋂C| = ⌊300/21⌋= 14
|B⋂C| = ⌊300/35⌋= 8
|A⋂B⋂C| = ⌊300/105⌋= 2
(3 Marks)
Then answer to the first question is
|A⋃B⋃C| = |A| + |B| + |C| −|B⋂A| −|A⋂C| −|B⋂C| + |B⋂A⋂C|
|A⋃B⋃C| = 100 + 60 + 42 −20 −14 −8 + 2
|A⋃B⋃C| = 162
(3 Marks)
The answer to the second question is 300 – 162 = 138.
(2 Marks)
Students can solve this question by Venn diagram also.

Q.5 c) Construct minimum spanning tree using Prim’s algorithm. Also write the steps of the
algorithm and find minimum total weight.
Solution: Here is the algorithm:
1) Set S = .
1) Pick any vertex in the graph. Update S
2) Add the minimum edge incident to that vertex to S.
3) Continue to add edges into S (n-2 more times) using the
following rule:
Add the minimum edge weight to S that is incident to S
but that doesn't form a cycle when added to S.
Total weight = 17

Question 7(c) (i)

Determine whether the given graph has an Eulerian path or an Eulerian circuit. If an Eulerian path or
circuit exists, write one such path or circuit explicitly. ‘

Solution

Let G be the given graph, then V(G) = {z,y, 2,8,u,v,w}. It can be seen that deg(x) = 2,deg(y) =
3, deg(z) = 4, deg(s) = 3, deg(u) = 4, deg(v) = 2,deg(w) = 2. We know that A connected graph with at
least two vertices has an Eulerian circuit if and only if each of its vertices has even degree. As Gisa Q mor fey |
connected graph (every pair of vertices can be reached from the other by traversing a sequence of edges) !
and there are two vertices of odd degree (y and s), the graph G can not have Eulerian circuit. J

Further, A connected graph has an Eulerian path but not an Eulerian circuit if and only if it has] j

exactly two vertices of odd degree. As G has exactly two vertices of odd degree, there exists an Eulerian !
path (which is not Eulerian circuit). am aly

If we consider the path P = (y,2,2,y, 8,2, u,v, w, 8) then, it covers all of the edges of G exactly

once (Path P has different starting and terminating vertices). Consequently, path P is an Eulerian path
which is not Eulerian circuit. 4

Question 7(c) (ii)

Provide an example of a graph that possesses both an Eulerian circuit and a Hamiltonian circuit, and
explain why the graph satisfies the conditions for both types of circuits. Additionally, give a non-trivial
example of a graph that has neither an Eulerian circuit nor a Hamiltonian circuit, and explain-clearly
why it fails to meet the requirements for both circuits. -

Solution

A graph G said to have an Eulerian circuit if there exists a simple circuit which covers all the edges (i.e.
a closed path which covers all the edges of graph G exactly once) and a Hamiltonian circuit in a: graph
G is a simple circuit that passes through every vertex of G exactly once.

i. Let's prove that this set of matrices forms a group under matrix multiplication and   6
whether the group is abelian (i.e., commutative).
Step 1: Closure
For closure, we need to show that the product of any two matrices in the set is also a
matrix of the same form.
Let’s multiply two such matrices M(\alpha)(α) and (β), where \alphaα and \beta are two
real numbers:
Page 8 of 20
--- page 9 ---
M(\alpha) \cdot M(\beta) = \begin{pmatrix} \cos(\alpha) & -\sin(\alpha) \\ \sin(\alpha) &
\cos(\alpha) \end{pmatrix} \cdot \begin{pmatrix} \cos(\beta) & -\sin(\beta) \\ \sin(\beta)
& \cos(\beta) \end{pmatrix}
Multiplying the matrices, we get:
M(\alpha) \cdot M(\beta) = \begin{pmatrix} \cos(\alpha)\cos(\beta) -
\sin(\alpha)\sin(\beta) & -\cos(\alpha)\sin(\beta) - \sin(\alpha)\cos(\beta) \\
\sin(\alpha)\cos(\beta) + \cos(\alpha)\sin(\beta) & -\sin(\alpha)\sin(\beta) +
\cos(\alpha)\cos(\beta) \end{pmatrix}
Using trigonometric identities:
\cos(\alpha + \beta) = \cos(\alpha)\cos(\beta) - \sin(\alpha)\sin(\beta)
\sin(\alpha + \beta) = \sin(\alpha)\cos(\beta) + \cos(\alpha)\sin(\beta)
Thus, the product is:
M(\alpha + \beta) = \begin{pmatrix} \cos(\alpha + \beta) & -\sin(\alpha + \beta) \\
\sin(\alpha + \beta) & \cos(\alpha + \beta) \end{pmatrix}
This is still a matrix of the same form, with
β as the new angle. Hence, the set is closed under matrix multiplication.   1.5 Marks
Step 2: Associativity
Matrix multiplication is associative in general. For any three matrices A, BB, and CC,
we have:
Thus, the set is associative under matrix multiplication.
1 Mark
Step 3: Identity Element
The identity matrix II in this set is the rotation matrix where \alpha = 0α=0:
Thus, M(0)M(0) is the identity element for this group.
1 Mark
Step 4: Inverse Element
The inverse of a matrix (α) is another matrix in the set. To find the inverse, we look for
(−α), since:
M(\alpha) \cdot M(-\alpha) = M(0) = I
Using trigonometric identities:
M(-\alpha) = \begin{pmatrix} \cos(-\alpha) & -\sin(-\alpha) \\ \sin(-\alpha) & \cos(-
\alpha) \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} \cos(\alpha) & \sin(\alpha) \\ -\sin(\alpha) &
\cos(\alpha) \end{pmatrix}
Hence,M(α) has an inverse M(−α), which is also a matrix of the same form.   1
Mark
Step 5: Commutativity (Abelian Property)
To check if the group is abelian, we need to verify whether matrix multiplication is
commutative. Let's calculate AM(\alpha) \cdot M(\beta)(α)⋅A(β) and A
M(\beta) \cdot M(\alpha)(β)⋅A(α):
M(\alpha) \cdot M(\beta) = M(\alpha + \beta)
Page 9 of 20
--- page 10 ---
